# Glossary of classical algebraic geometry

## o1 subject: Glossary of classical algebraic geometry

The terminology of algebraic geometry changed drastically during the twentieth century, with the introduction of the general methods, initiated by David Hilbert and the Italian school of algebraic geometry in the beginning of the century, and later formalized by André Weil, Jean-Pierre Serre and Alexander Grothendieck. Much of the classical terminology, mainly based on case study, was simply abandoned, with the result that books and papers written before this time can be hard to read. This article lists some of this classical terminology, and describes some of the changes in conventions.

## o2 citation

## o3 citation

## o4 citation

## o5 citation

## o6 citation

## o7 citation

## o8 citation

## o9 section: Conventions

## o10 definition/notation: Conventions

## Conventions
The change in terminology from around 1948 to 1960 is not the only difficulty in understanding classical algebraic geometry. There was also a lot of background knowledge and assumptions, much of which has now changed. This section lists some of these changes.
- In classical algebraic geometry, adjectives were often used as nouns: for example, "quartic" could also be short for "quartic curve" or "quartic surface".
- In classical algebraic geometry, all curves, surfaces, varieties, and so on came with fixed embeddings into projective space, whereas in scheme theory they are more often considered as abstract varieties. For example, a Veronese surface was not just a copy of the projective plane, but a copy of the projective plane together with an embedding into projective 5-space.
- Varieties were often considered only up to birational isomorphism, whereas in scheme theory they are usually considered up to biregular isomorphism.
- Until circa 1950, many of the proofs in classical algebraic geometry were incomplete (or occasionally just wrong). In particular authors often did not bother to check degenerate cases.
- Words (such as azygetic or bifid) were sometimes formed from Latin or Greek roots without further explanation, assuming that readers would use their classical education to figure out the meaning.
- Definitions in classical algebraic geometry were often somewhat vague, and it is futile to try to find the precise meaning of some of the older terms because many of them never had a precise meaning. In practice this did not matter much when the terms were only used to describe particular examples, as in these cases their meaning was usually clear: for example, it was obvious what the 16 tropes of a Kummer surface were, even if "trope" was not precisely defined in general.
- Algebraic geometry was often implicitly done over the complex numbers (or sometimes the real numbers).
- Readers were often assumed to know classical (or synthetic) projective geometry, and in particular to have a thorough knowledge of conics, and authors would use terminology from this area without further explanation.
- Several terms, such as "Abelian group", "complete", "complex", "flat", "harmonic", "homology", "monoid", "normal", "pole", "regular", now have meanings that are unrelated to their original meanings. Other terms, such as "circle", have their meanings tacitly changed to work in complex projective space; for example, a circle in complex algebraic geometry is a conic passing through the circular points at infinity and has underlying topological space a 2-sphere rather than a 1-sphere.
- Sometimes capital letters are tacitly understood to stand for points, and small letters for lines or curves.

## o14 section: Symbols

## o15 entry: [1], [2], . . . , [n]

[1], [2], . . . , [n]
Projective space of dimension $1, 2, \ldots, n$. This notation was introduced by .

## o16 citation

## o17 entry: ∞¹, ∞², ...

∞¹, ∞², ...
A family of dimension 1, 2, ...

## o18 entry: {1}, {2}, ...,{n}

{1}, {2}, ...,{n}
A family or variety of dimension $1, 2, \ldots, n$.

## o19 citation

## o20 section: A

## o21 entry: Abelian group

Abelian group
An archaic name for the symplectic group.
A commutative group.

## o22 entry: aberrancy

aberrancy
The deviation of a curve from circular form. See .

## o23 citation

## o24 entry: absolute

absolute
A fixed choice of something in projective space, used to construct some other geometry from projective geometry. For example, choosing a plane, called the absolute plane, of projective space can be used to make its complement into a copy of affine space. Choosing a suitable conic or polarity, called the Cayley absolute, absolute conic or absolute polarity, in the absolute plane provides the means to put a metric on affine space so that it becomes a metric space.
Absolute geometry is roughly Euclidean geometry without the parallel postulate.

## o25 entry: accidental

accidental
An accidental (or improper) double point of a surface in 4-dimensional projective space is a double point with two distinct tangent planes.

## o26 citation

## o27 entry: acnode

acnode
An acnode is an isolated point of a real curve. See .

## o28 citation

## o29 entry: adjoint

adjoint
If C is a curve, an adjoint of C is a curve such that any point of C of multiplicity r has multiplicity at least r–1 on the adjoint. Sometimes the multiple points of C are required to be ordinary, and if theis condition is not satisfied the term "sub-adjoint" is used.

## o30 citation

## o31 entry: affine

affine
Affine space is roughly a vector space where one has forgotten which point is the origin.
An affine variety is a variety in affine space.

## o32 entry: affinity

affinity
An automorphism of affine space.

## o33 entry: aggregate

aggregate
A set.

## o34 entry: ambient

ambient
An ambient variety is a large variety containing all the points, curves, divisors, and so on that one is interested in.

## o35 entry: anharmonic ratio

anharmonic ratio
Cross-ratio

## o36 entry: antipoint

antipoint
One of a pair of points constructed from two foci of a curve. See .

## o37 citation

## o38 entry: apparent

apparent
An apparent singularity is a singularity of a projection of a variety into a hyperplane. They are so called because they appear to be singularities to an observer at the point being projected from.

## o39 citation

## o40 entry: apolar

apolar
Orthogonal under the polar pairing between the symmetric algebra of a vector space and its dual.

## o41 entry: arithmetic genus

arithmetic genus
The arithmetic genus of a variety is a variation of the Euler characteristic of the trivial line bundle; see Hodge number.

## o42 entry: Aronhold set

Aronhold set
One of the 288 sets of 7 of the 28 bitangents of a quartic curve corresponding to the 7 odd theta characteristics of a normal set.

## o43 entry: associated

associated
An associated curve is the image of a projective curve in a Grassmannian, given by taking the tangent lines, or osculating planes, and so on.

## o44 entry: axial

axial

## o45 entry: axis

axis
A special line or linear subspace associated with some family of geometric objects. For example, a special linear complex in 4-dimensional space consists of all lines meeting a given plane, that is called the axial plane of the complex. Similar to directrix.

## o46 citation

## o47 entry: azygetic

azygetic
Unpaired. Opposite of syzygetic, meaning paired. Example: azygetic triad, azygetic tetrad, azygetic set.

## o48 section: B

## o49 entry: base

base
A base point is a point common to all members of a family.
The base number ρ is the rank of the Neron–Severi group.

## o50 entry: bicircular

bicircular
Having nodes at the two circular points at infinity, as in bicircular curve. See .

## o51 citation

## o52 entry: bicorn

bicorn
A bicorn is a curve with two cusps.

## o53 entry: bicuspidal

bicuspidal
Having two cusps

## o54 entry: bidegree

bidegree
A pair of integers giving the degrees of a bihomogeneous polynomial in two sets of variables

## o55 entry: bielliptic

bielliptic
A bielliptic curve is a branched double cover of an elliptic curve.
A bielliptic surface is the same as a hyperelliptic surface.

## o56 entry: bifid

bifid
Split into two equal parts
A bifid map is an element of the vector space of dimension 2g over the field with 2 elements, consisting of the 2g+1-dimensional space of even-cardinality subsets of a set S of 2+2g elements, modulo the 1-dimensional space {0,S}.
A bifid substitution is a permutation of the 28 bitangents of a quartic curve depending on one of the 35 decompositions of 8 symbols into two sets of 4 symbols. See .

## o57 citation

## o58 citation

## o59 entry: biflecnode

biflecnode
Same as fleflecnode. See .

## o60 citation

## o61 entry: bigenus

bigenus
The second plurigenus P2 of a surface.

## o62 entry: bihomogeneous

bihomogeneous
Homogeneous in each of two sets of variables, as in bihomogeneous form.

## o63 entry: binary

binary
Depending on two variables, as in binary form

## o64 entry: binodal

binodal
Having two nodes

## o65 entry: binode

binode
A double point of a surface whose tangent cone consists of two different planes. See unode.

## o66 citation

## o67 entry: bipartite

bipartite
Having two connected components. See .

## o68 citation

## o69 entry: bipunctual

bipunctual
Having two points
For a bipunctual conic with respect to 3 points see .

## o70 citation

## o71 entry: birational

birational
Two varieties are birational if they are isomorphic off lower-dimensional subsets
A birational map is a rational map with rational "inverse"

## o72 entry: biregular

biregular
A biregular map is a regular map with regular inverse
Two varieties are biregular if there is a biregular map from one to the other, in other words if they are isomorphic as abstract varieties.

## o73 entry: biscribed

biscribed
Both circumscribed and inscribed, or in other words having vertices that lie on a curve and sides that are tangent to the curve, as in biscribed triangle.

## o74 citation

## o75 entry: bitangent

bitangent
A bitangent is a line that is tangent to a curve at two points. See .

## o76 citation

## o77 entry: bitangential

bitangential
Meeting a curve at the tangency points of its bitangents

## o78 entry: Brianchon hexagon

Brianchon hexagon
A non-planar hexagon whose three diagonals meet.

## o79 citation

## o80 section: C

## o81 entry: canonical

canonical
The canonical series is the linear series of the canonical line bundle
The canonical bundle is the line bundle of differential forms of highest degree.
The canonical map or canonical embedding is the map to the projective space of the sections of the canonical bundle
A canonical curve (or variety) is the image of a curve (or variety) under the canonical map
The canonical class is the divisor class of a canonical divisor
A canonical divisor is a divisor of a section of the canonical line bundle.

## o82 entry: Canonizant

Canonizant
A canonizant is a covariant of forms.

## o83 entry: catalecticant

catalecticant
A catalecticant is an invariant of a binary form of degree 2n that vanishes when the form is a sum of powers of n linear forms.

## o84 entry: caustic

caustic
A caustic is the envelope of light rays from a point reflected in a curve

## o85 entry: Cayley

Cayley

## o86 entry: Cayleyan

Cayleyan
Named after Arthur Cayley
See
A Cayley octad is a set of 8 points in projective space given by the intersection of three quadrics.
The Cayley lines or Cayley–Salmon lines are the 20 lines passing through 3 Kirkman points.
A Cayley absolute is a conic or quadric used to define a metric.

## o87 citation

## o88 citation

## o89 entry: center

center

## o90 entry: centre

centre
A special point associated with some geometric object
The center of a perspectivity
The center of an isologue

## o91 entry: character

character

## o92 entry: characteristic

characteristic
An integer associated with a projective variety, such as its degree, rank, order, class, type. In particular the Plücker characteristics of a curve are the order, class, number of nodes, number of bitangents, number of cusps, and number of inflections.
A characteristic exponent is an exponent of a power series with non-negative coefficient, that is not divisible by the highest common factor of preceding exponents with non-zero coefficients.
The characteristic series of a linear system of divisors on a surface is the linear system of 0-cycles on one of the divisors given by its intersections with the other divisors.

## o93 citation

## o94 citation

## o95 citation

## o96 entry: chord

chord
A line joining two points of a variety

## o97 entry: chordal variety

chordal variety
A chordal variety is the union of the chords and tangent spaces of a projective variety

## o98 entry: circle

circle
A plane conic passing through the circular points at infinity. For real projective geometry this is much the same as a circle in the usual sense, but for complex projective geometry it is different: for example, circles have underlying topological spaces given by a 2-sphere rather than a 1-sphere.

## o99 entry: circuit

circuit
A component of a real algebraic curve. A circuit is called even or odd depending on whether it has an even or odd number of intersections with a generic line.

## o100 citation

## o101 entry: circular

circular
A circular point is one of the two points at infinity (1: i: 0), (1: −i: 0) through which all circles pass
A circular algebraic curve is a curve passing through the two circular points at infinity. See also bicircular.

## o102 entry: circumscribed

circumscribed
Having edges tangent to some curve, as in circumscribed quadrilateral.
Passing through the vertices of something, as in circumscribed circle.

## o103 entry: cissoid

cissoid
A cissoid is the curve generated from two curves and a point. See .

## o104 citation

## o105 entry: class

class
The class of a plane curve is the number of proper tangents passing through a generic point of the plane.
The class of a space curve is the number of osculating planes passing through a generic point of space.
The class of a surface in rdimensional projective space is the number of tangent planes meeting a generic codimension 2 subspace in a line.
The degree of a contravariant or concomitant in the covariant variables.

## o106 citation

## o107 citation

## o108 citation

## o109 entry: coaxal

coaxal

## o110 entry: coaxial

coaxial
A pencil of circles is called coaxal if their centers all lie on a line (called the axis).
A family of plane circles all passing through the same two points (other than the circular points at infinity).

## o111 citation

## o112 entry: coincidence

coincidence
A coincidence quadric is a quadric associated to a correlation, given by the locus of points lying in the corresponding hyperplane.
A fixed point of a correspondence, in other words a point of a variety corresponding to itself under a correspondence.

## o113 citation

## o114 citation

## o115 entry: collinear

collinear
On the same line

## o116 entry: collineation

collineation
A collineation is an isomorphism from one projective space to another, often to itself. See correlation.

## o117 citation

## o118 entry: complete

complete
A linear series of divisors is called complete if it is not contained in a larger linear series.
A scheme is called complete if the map to a point is proper
A complete quadrangle is 4 points and the 6 lines joining pairs
A complete quadrilateral is 4 lines meeting in pairs in 6 points
A complete conic in the plane is a (possibly degenerate) conic, together with a pair of (possibly equal) points on it if it is a double line

## o119 citation

## o120 entry: complex

complex
(Noun.) A line complex, a family of lines of codimension 1 in the family of all lines in some projective space, in particular a 3-dimensional family of lines in 3-dimensional projective space. See congruence.
(Adjective.) Related to the complex numbers.
The (line) complex group is an old name for the symplectic group.

## o121 citation

## o122 entry: composite

composite
Reducible (meaning having more than one irreducible component).

## o123 entry: conchoid

conchoid
A conchoid is the curve given by the cissoid of a circle and another curve. See .

## o124 citation

## o125 entry: concomitant

concomitant
A (mixed) concomitant is an invariant homogeneous polynomial in the coefficients of a form, a covariant variable, and a contravariant variable. In other words it is a (tri)homogeneous polynomial on SV⊕V⊕V* for some vector space V, where SV is some symmetric power of V and V* its dual, that is invariant under the special linear group of V. In practice V often has dimension 2. The degree, class, and order of a concomitant are its degrees in the three types of variable. Concomitants are generalizations of covariants, contravariants, and invariants.

## o126 entry: concurrent

concurrent
Meeting at a point

## o127 entry: cone

cone
The union of the lines joining an algebraic set with a linear algebraic set. Called a point-cone, line-cone, ... if the linear set is a point, line, ...
A subset of a vector space closed under multiplication by scalars.

## o128 citation

## o129 entry: configuration

configuration
A configuration is a finite set of points and lines (and sometimes planes), generally with equal numbers of points per line and equal numbers of lines per point.

## o130 entry: confocal

confocal
Having the same foci

## o131 entry: congruence

congruence
A family of lines in projective space such that there are a nonzero finite number of lines through a generic point . See complex.

## o132 citation

## o133 entry: conic

conic
A conic is a degree 2 curve. Short for "conic section", the intersection of a cone with a plane.

## o134 entry: conjugate

conjugate
A conjugate point is an acnode.
A conjugate point is a point lying on the hyperplane corresponding to another point under a polarity.
A conjugate line is a line containing the point corresponding to another line under a polarity (or plane conic).
For harmonic conjugate see harmonic.

## o135 citation

## o136 citation

## o137 entry: connex

connex
A correspondence between a projective space and its dual.

## o138 entry: consecutive

consecutive
Infinitesimally near. For example, a tangent line to a curve is a line through two consecutive points of the curve, and a focal point is the intersection of the normals of two consecutive points.

## o139 entry: contravariant

contravariant
A bihomogeneous polynomial in dual variables of x, y, ... and the coefficients of some homogeneous form in x, y,... that is invariant under some group of linear transformations. In other words it is a bihomogeneous polynomial on SV⊕V for some vector space V, where SV is some symmetric power of V and V* its dual, that is invariant under the special linear group of V. In practice V often has dimension at least 3, because when it has dimension 2 these are more or less the same as covariants. The degree and class of a contravariant are its degrees in the two types of variable. Contravariants generalize invariants and are special cases of concomitants, and are in some sense dual to covariants.

## o140 entry: coplanar

coplanar
In the same plane

## o141 entry: correlation

correlation
An isomorphism from a projective space to the dual of a projective space, often to the dual of itself. A correlation on the projective space of a vector space is essentially the same as a nonsingular bilinear form on the vector space, up to multiplication by constants.

## o142 citation

## o143 entry: coresidual

coresidual
See

## o144 citation

## o145 entry: correspondence

correspondence
A correspondence from X to Y is an algebraic subset of X×Y

## o146 entry: cosingular

cosingular
Having the same singularities

## o147 entry: couple

couple
An ordered pair

## o148 entry: covariant

covariant
A bihomogeneous polynomial in x, y, ... and the coefficients of some homogeneous form in x, y,... that is invariant under some group of linear transformations. In other words it is a bihomogeneous polynomial on SV⊕V* for some vector space V, where SV is some symmetric power of V and V* its dual, that is invariant under the special linear group of V. In practice V often has dimension 2. The degree and order of a covariant are its degrees in the two types of variable. Covariants generalize invariants and are special cases of concomitants, and are in some sense dual to contravariants
The variety defined by a covariant. In particular the curve defined by the Hessian or Steinerian covariants of a curve are called covariant curves.

## o149 citation

## o150 entry: Cremona transformation

Cremona transformation
A Cremona transformation is a birational map from a projective space to itself

## o151 entry: cross-ratio

cross-ratio
The cross-ratio is an invariant of 4 points on a projective line.

## o152 entry: crunode

crunode
Crunode is an archaic term for a node, a double point with distinct tangent directions.

## o153 entry: cubic

cubic
Degree 3, especially a degree 3 projective variety

## o154 entry: cubo-cubic

cubo-cubic
A cubo-cubic transformation is a Cremona transformation such that the homaloids of the transformation and its inverse all have degree 3.

## o155 citation

## o156 entry: curve

curve
A curve together with an embedding into projective space.

## o157 entry: cusp

cusp
A cusp is a singular point of a curve whose tangent cone is a line.

## o158 entry: cuspidal edge

cuspidal edge
The locus of the focal points of a family of planes

## o159 citation

## o160 entry: cyclide

cyclide
A cyclide is a quartic surface passing doubly through the absolute conic.

## o161 citation

## o162 section: D

## o163 entry: decic

decic

## o164 entry: decimic

decimic
(Adjective) Degree 10
(Noun) A degree 10 projective variety

## o165 entry: deficiency

deficiency
The deficiency of a linear system is its codimension in the corresponding complete linear system.
The deficiency D of a plane curve is an approximation to its genus, equal to the genus when all singular points are ordinary, given by (n–1)(n–2)/2 –(a–1)(a–2)/2 – (b–1)(b–2)/2 –..., where n is the degree of the curve and a. b, ... are the multiplicities of its singular points. ,

## o166 citation

## o167 citation

## o168 entry: degree

degree
The number of intersection points of a projective variety with a generic linear subspace of complementary dimension
The number of points of a divisor on a curve

## o169 entry: Desargues

Desargues
The Desargues figure or configuration is a configuration of 10 lines and 10 points in Desargues' theorem.

## o170 entry: desmic system

desmic system
A desmic system is a configuration of three desmic tetrahedra.

## o171 entry: developable

developable
(Noun) A 1-dimensional family of planes in 3-dimensional projective space .
(Noun) The envelope of the normals of a curve
(Noun) Short for a developable surface, one that can be unrolled to a plane
The tangent developable of a curve is the surface consisting of its tangent lines.
Flat, as in developable surface

## o172 citation

## o173 entry: differential

differential
A differential of the first kind is a holomorphic 1-form.
A differential of the second kind is a meromorphic 1-form such that the residues of all poles are 0. Sometimes it is only allowed to have one pole that must be of order 2.
A differential of the third kind is sometimes a meromorphic 1-form such that all poles are simple (order 1). Sometimes it is only allowed to have 2 poles.

## o174 entry: director

director
The director circle of a conic is the locus of points where two orthogonal tangent lines to the conic meet. More generally the director conic of a conic in regard to two points is defined in a similar way.

## o175 citation

## o176 entry: directrix

directrix
A straight line, or more generally a projective space, associated with some geometric configuration, such as the directrix of a conic section or the directrix of a rational normal scroll

## o177 entry: discriminant

discriminant
The invariant (on the vector space of forms of degree d in n variables) which vanishes exactly when the corresponding hypersurface in Pn-1 is singular.

## o178 entry: double curve

double curve
A 1-dimensional singularity, usually of a surface, of multiplicity 2

## o179 entry: double point

double point
A 0-dimensional singularity of multiplicity 2, such as a node.
One of the two points fixed by an involution of a projective line.

## o180 citation

## o181 entry: double six

double six
The Schläfli double six configuration

## o182 entry: duad

duad
A set of two points

## o183 entry: dual

dual
The dual of a projective space is the set of hyperplanes, considered as another projective space.
The dual curve of a plane curve is the set of its tangent lines, considered as a curve in the dual projective plane.
A dual number is a number of the form a+εb where ε has square 0.

## o184 citation

## o185 section: E

## o186 entry: Eckardt point

Eckardt point
An Eckardt point is a point of intersection of 3 lines on a cubic surface.

## o187 entry: effective

effective
An effective cycle or divisor is one with no negative coefficients

## o188 entry: elation

elation
A collineation that fixes all points on a line (called its axis) and all lines though a point on the axis (called its center).

## o189 entry: eleven-point conic

eleven-point conic
The eleven-point conic is a conic containing 11 special points associated to four points and a line.

## o190 citation

## o191 entry: embedded

embedded
An embedded variety is one contained in a larger variety, sometimes called the ambient variety.

## o192 entry: enneaedro

enneaedro
A set of 9 tritangent planes to a cubic surface containing the 27 lines.

## o193 entry: envelope

envelope
A curve tangent to a family of curves. See .

## o194 citation

## o195 entry: epitrochoid

epitrochoid
An epitrochoid is the curve traced by a point of a disc rolling along another disc.

## o196 citation

## o197 entry: equiaffine

equiaffine

## o198 entry: equiaffinity

equiaffinity
An equiaffinity is an equiaffine transformation, meaning an affine transformation preserving area.

## o199 entry: equianharmonic

equianharmonic
Four points whose cross ratio (or anharmonic ratio) is a cube root of 1
An equianharmonic cubic is a cubic curve with j-invariant 0

## o200 entry: equivalence

equivalence
In intersection theory, a positive-dimensional variety sometimes behaves formally as if it were a finite number of points; this number is called its equivalence.

## o201 entry: evectant

evectant
A contravariant defined by Sylvester depending on an invariant. See .

## o202 citation

## o203 entry: evolute

evolute
An evolute is the envelope of the normal lines of a plane curve. See .

## o204 citation

## o205 entry: exceptional

exceptional
Corresponding to something of lower dimension under a birational correspondence, as in exceptional curve, exceptional divisor
An exceptional curve on a surface is one that corresponds to a simple point on another surface under a birational correspondence. It is called an exceptional curve of the first kind if it is transformed into a point of the other surface, and an exceptional curve of the second kind if it is transformed into a curve of the other surface.

## o206 section: F

## o207 entry: facultative

facultative
A facultative point is one where a given function is positive.

## o208 citation

## o209 entry: first kind

first kind
holomorphic or regular (when applied to differentials)

## o210 entry: flat

flat
(Noun) A linear subspace of projective space, such as a point, line, plane, hyperplane.
(Adjective) Having curvature zero.
(Adjective) For the term "flat" in scheme theory see flat module, flat morphism.

## o211 entry: flecnode

flecnode
A double point that is also a point of inflexion of one branch. .

## o212 citation

## o213 citation

## o214 entry: fleflecnode

fleflecnode
A double point that is also a point of inflexion of both branches. .

## o215 citation

## o216 entry: flex

flex
Short for point of inflection

## o217 entry: focal

focal
A focal point, line, plane, ... is the intersection of several consecutive elements of a family of linear subspaces.
A focal curve, surface and so on is the locus of the focal points of a family of linear subspaces.

## o218 citation

## o219 citation

## o220 entry: focus

focus
A focal point. See ,

## o221 citation

## o222 citation

## o223 entry: foliate singularity

foliate singularity
See

## o224 citation

## o225 entry: form

form
A homogeneous polynomial in several variables. Same as quantic.
A differential form.

## o226 entry: free intersection

free intersection
An intersection point of two members of a family that is not a base point.

## o227 entry: freedom

freedom
Dimension, as in degrees of freedom. .

## o228 citation

## o229 entry: fundamental

fundamental
This term seem to be ambiguous and poorly defined: Zariski states: "I can find no clear-cut definition of a fundamental curve in the literature".
The fundamental set or fundamental locus of a birational correspondence appears to mean (roughly) either the set of points where it is not a bijection or the set of points where it is not defined.
A fundamental point, curve, or variety is a point, curve, or variety in the fundamental set of a birational correspondence.

## o230 section: G

## o231 entry: g

g g${}^{r}_{d}$, γ${}^{r}_{d}$
A linear or algebraic system of divisors of dimension r and degree d on a curve. The letter g is used for linear systems, and the letter γ is used for algebraic systems.

## o232 entry: generator

generator
One of the lines of a ruled surface or more generally an element of some family of linear spaces.

## o233 citation

## o234 citation

## o235 entry: generic

generic
Not having some special properties, which are usually not stated explicitly.
A generic point is one having coordinates that are algebraically independent over the base field.
The generic point of a scheme.

## o236 entry: genus

genus
The dimension of the space of sections of the canonical bundle, as in the genus of a curve or the geometric genus of a surface
arithmetic genus of a surface
plurigenus

## o237 entry: geometric genus

geometric genus
The geometric genus is the dimension of the space of holomorphic n-forms on an n-dimensional non-singular projective variety.

## o238 entry: grade

grade
The grade of a linear system of divisors on an n-dimensional variety is the number of free intersection points of n generic divisors. In particular the grade of a linear series of divisors on a curve is now called the degree and is the number of points in each divisor , and the grade of a net of curves on a surface is the number of free intersections of two generic curves.

## o239 citation

## o240 citation

## o241 citation

## o242 entry: Grassmannian

Grassmannian
A Grassmannian is a variety parameterizing linear subspaces of projective space

## o243 entry: group

group
A group or point-group is an archaic term for an effective divisor on a curve. This usage is particularly confusing, because some such divisors are called normal, with the result that there are "normal sub-groups" having nothing to do with the normal subgroups of group theory.
A group in the usual sense.

## o244 citation

## o245 section: H

## o246 entry: harmonic

harmonic
Two pairs of points on a line are harmonic if their cross ratio is –1. The 4 points are called a harmonic set, and the points of one pair are called harmonic conjugates with respect to the other pair.
A harmonic cubic is an elliptic curve with j-invariant 1728, given by a double cover of the projective line branched at 4 points with cross ratio –1.
Satisfying some analogue of the Laplace equation, as in harmonic form.
The harmonic polar line of an inflection point of a cubic curve is the component of the polar conic other than the tangent line.
A harmonic net is a set of points on a line containing the harmonic conjugate of any point with respect to any other two points.
For harmonically conjugate conics see .

## o247 citation

## o248 citation

## o249 citation

## o250 entry: Hesse

Hesse

## o251 entry: Hessian

Hessian
Named after Otto Hesse.
A Hessian matrix, or a variety associated with it. See .
The Hessian line is a line associated to 3 points A, B, C, of a conic, containing the three points given by the intersections of the tangents at A, B, C with the lines BC, CA, AB.
The Hessian point is a point associated to three lines tangent to a conic, whose construction is dual to that of a Hessian line.
The Hessian pair or Hessian duad of three points on a projective line is the pair of points fixed by the projective transformations of order 3 permuting the 3 points. More generally the Hessian pair is also defined in a similar way for triples of points of a rational curve, or triples of elements of a pencil.
The Hesse configuration is the configuration of inflection points of a plane cubic.
The Hesse group is the group of automorphisms of the Hesse configuration, of order 216.

## o252 citation

## o253 entry: hexad

hexad
A set of 6 points

## o254 entry: homaloid

homaloid
An element of a homaloidal system, in particular the image of a hyperlpane under a Cremona transformation.

## o255 entry: homaloidal

homaloidal
A homaloidal linear system of divisors is a linear system of grade 1, such as the image of the linear system of hyperplanes of projective space under a Cremona transformation. When the linear system has dimension 2 or 3 it is called a homaloidal net or homaloidal web.
Homaloidal means similar to a flat plane.

## o256 citation

## o257 citation

## o258 entry: homographic

homographic
Having the same invariants. See .
A homographic transformation is an automorphism of projective space over a field, in other words an element of the projective general linear group.

## o259 citation

## o260 citation

## o261 entry: homography

homography
An isomorphism between projective spaces induced by an isomorphism of vector spaces.
An axis of homography is a line associated to two related ranges of a conic.

## o262 citation

## o263 entry: homology

homology
As in homology group
A collineation fixing all lines through a point (the center) and all points through a line (the axis) not containing the center. See elation. This terminology was introduced by Lie.
An automorphism of projective space with a hyperplane of fixed points (called the axis). It is called a harmonic homology if it has order 2, in which case it has an isolated fixed point called its center.

## o264 entry: Hurwitz curve

Hurwitz curve

## o265 entry: Hurwitz surface

Hurwitz surface
A Hurwitz curve is a complex algebraic curve of genus g>0 with the maximum possible number 84(g–1) of automorphisms.

## o266 entry: hyperbolism

hyperbolism
Essentially a blow-up of a curve at a point. See .

## o267 citation

## o268 entry: hypercusp

hypercusp
A singularity of a curve of some multiplicity r whose tangent cone is a single line meeting the curve with order r+1.

## o269 citation

## o270 entry: hyperelliptic

hyperelliptic
A hyperelliptic curve is a curve with a degree 2 map to the projective line.

## o271 entry: hyperflex

hyperflex
Same as point of undulation: a point of a curve where the tangent line has contact of order at least 4.

## o272 entry: hyperosculating point

hyperosculating point
A point where the tangent space meets with order higher than normal.

## o273 entry: hyperplane

hyperplane
A linear subspace of projective space of codimension 1. Same as prime.

## o274 section: I

## o275 entry: index of speciality

index of speciality
The dimension of the first cohomology group of the line bundle of a divisor D; often denoted by i or i(D).

## o276 citation

## o277 entry: infinitely near point

infinitely near point
A point on a blow up of a variety

## o278 entry: inflection

inflection

## o279 entry: inflexion

inflexion
An inflection is a point where the curvature vanishes, or in other words where the tangent line meets with order at least 3. Differential geometry uses the slightly stricter condition that the curvature changes sign at the point. See

## o280 citation

## o281 entry: inpolar quadric

inpolar quadric
See

## o282 citation

## o283 entry: inscribed

inscribed
Having vertices on a curve, as in inscribed figure.
Tangent to some lines, as in inscribed circle.

## o284 entry: integral

integral
An integral of the first kind is a holomorphic closed differential form.
An integral of the second kind is a meromorphic closed differential form with no residues.
An integral of the third kind is a meromorphic closed differential form whose poles are all simple.
A simple integral is a closed 1-form, or the result of integrating a 1-form.
A double integral is a closed 2-form, or the result of integrating a 2-form.

## o285 entry: invariant

invariant
(Noun) A polynomial in the coefficients of a homogeneous form, invariant under some group of linear transformations. See also covariant, contravariant, concomitant.

## o286 entry: inversion

inversion
An inversion is a transformation of order 2 exchanging the inside and outside of a circle. See .

## o287 citation

## o288 entry: involute

involute
An involute is a curve obtained by unrolling a string around a curve. See .

## o289 citation

## o290 entry: involution

involution
A transformation whose square is the identity. Cremona transformations that are involutions include Bertini involutions, Geiser involutions, and De Jonquières involutions.

## o291 entry: irregularity

irregularity
The irregularity of a surface is the dimension of the space of holomorphic 1-forms on a non-singular projective surface; see Hodge number.

## o292 entry: isologue

isologue
Given a Cremoma transformation T, the isologue of a point p is the set of points x such that p, x, T(x) are collinear. The point p is called the center of the isologue.

## o293 section: J

## o294 entry: Jacobian

Jacobian
The Jacobian variety of a curve
A Jacobian curve; see below

## o295 entry: Jacobian curve

Jacobian curve
The locus of double points of curves of a net.

## o296 citation

## o297 entry: Jacobian set

Jacobian set
The set of free double points of a pencil of curves.

## o298 citation

## o299 entry: Jacobian system

Jacobian system
The linear system generated by Jacobian curves.

## o300 citation

## o301 entry: join

join
The join of two linear spaces is the smallest linear space containing both of them.

## o302 section: K

## o303 entry: kenotheme

kenotheme
An intersection of n hypersurfaces in n-dimensional projective space. Archaic.

## o304 citation

## o305 entry: keratoid

keratoid
Horn-like. A keratoid cusp is one whose two branches curve in opposite direction; see ramphoid cusp.

## o306 citation

## o307 entry: Kirkman point

Kirkman point
One of the 60 points lying on 3 of the Plücker lines associated with 6 points on a conic.

## o308 entry: Klein

Klein
Felix Klein
The Klein icosahedral surface is a certain cubic surface
The Klein quartic is the curve $x^3y + y^3z + z^3x = 0.$

## o309 entry: Kronecker index

Kronecker index
The intersection number of two curves on a surface

## o310 entry: Kummer surface

Kummer surface
A quartic surface with 16 nodes

## o311 section: L

## o312 entry: Laguerre net

Laguerre net
A net V of plane curves of some degree d such that the base locus of a generic pencil of V is the base locus of V together with d–1 collinear points

## o313 citation

## o314 citation

## o315 entry: lemniscate

lemniscate
A lemniscate is a curve resembling a figure 8. See

## o316 citation

## o317 entry: limaçon

limaçon
A limaçon is a curve traced by a point on a circle rolling around a similar circle. See

## o318 citation

## o319 entry: line

line
A line in projective space; in other words a subvariety of degree 1 and dimension 1.

## o320 entry: line coordinates

line coordinates
Projective coordinates. See

## o321 citation

## o322 entry: linear

linear
Degree 1

## o323 entry: linear system

linear system
A linear system of divisors, given by the zeros of elements of a vector space of sections of a line bundle

## o324 entry: locus

locus
1-A subset of projective space given by points satisfying some condition

## o325 section: M

## o326 entry: manifold

manifold
An algebraic manifold is a cycle of projective space, in other words a formal linear combination of irreducible subvarieties. Algebraic manifolds may have singularities, so their underlying topological spaces need not be manifolds in the sense of differential topology.

## o327 citation

## o328 entry: meet

meet
The meet of two sets is their intersection.

## o329 entry: Möbius tetrads

Möbius tetrads
Two tetrads such that the plane containing any three points of one tetrad contains a point of the other.

## o330 citation

## o331 entry: model

model
A variety whose points (or sometimes hyperplane sections) correspond to elements of some family. Similar to what is now called a parameter space or moduli space.
A model for a field extension K of a field k is a projective variety over k together with an isomorphism between K and its field of rational functions.

## o332 entry: modulus

modulus
A function of algebraic varieties depending only on the isomorphism type; in other words, a function on a moduli space

## o333 entry: Moebius tetrads

Moebius tetrads
See Möbius tetrads

## o334 entry: monoid

monoid
A surface of degree n with a point of multiplicity n–1.

## o335 citation

## o336 entry: monoidal transformation

monoidal transformation
A Cremona transformation of projective space generated by a family of monoids with the same point of multiplicity n–1. More generally a blow-up along a subvariety, called the center of the monoidal transformation.

## o337 citation

## o338 entry: multiple

multiple
A multiple point is a singular point (one with a non-regular local ring).

## o339 entry: multiplicity

multiplicity
The multiplicity of a point on a hypersurface is the degree of the first non-vanishing coefficient of the Taylor series at the point. More generally one can define the multiplicity of any point of a variety as the multiplicity of its local ring. A point has multiplicity 1 if and only if it is non-singular.

## o340 section: N

## o341 entry: Néron–Severi group

Néron–Severi group
The Néron–Severi group is the group of divisors module numerical equivalence.

## o342 entry: nest

nest
Two components (circuits) of a real algebraic curve are said to nest if one is inside the other.

## o343 citation

## o344 entry: net

net
A 2-dimensional linear system. See "pencil" and "web". See also Laguerre net.
A harmonic net is a set of points on a line containing the harmonic conjugate of any point with respect to any other two points.

## o345 citation

## o346 entry: Newton polygon

Newton polygon
The convex hull of the points with coordinates given by the exponents of the terms of a polynomial.

## o347 entry: nodal

nodal
A nodal tangent to a singular point of a curve is one of the lines of its tangent cone.

## o348 citation

## o349 entry: node

node
A singular point p of a hypersurface f = 0, usually with the determinant of the Hessian of f not zero at p.

## o350 citation

## o351 entry: node cusp

node cusp
A singularity of a curve where a node and a cusp coincide at the same point.

## o352 citation

## o353 entry: normal

normal
A subvariety of projective space is linearly normal if the linear system defining the embedding is complete; see rational normal curve.
Orthogonal to the tangent space, such as a line orthogonal to the tangent space or the normal bundle.
A normal intersection is an intersection with the "expected" codimension (given a sum of codimensions).
Local rings are integrally closed; see normal scheme.

## o354 citation

## o355 entry: null-polarity

null-polarity
A correlation given by a skew symmetric matrix. A null-polarity of the projective space of a vector space is essentially a non-degenerate skew-symmetric bilinear form, up to multiplication by scalars. See also polarity.

## o356 citation

## o357 section: O

## o358 entry: octad

octad
A set of 8 points

## o359 entry: octic

octic
(Adjective) Degree 8
(Noun) A degree 8 projective variety

## o360 entry: ombilic

ombilic
The curve at infinity which is the intersection of any sphere with the plane at infinity. All points of the ombilic are non-real.

## o361 entry: order

order
Now called degree of an algebraic variety: the number of intersection points with a generic linear subspace of complementary dimension.
The order of a covariant or concomitant: its degree in the contravariant variables.
The order of a Cremona transformation is the order (degree) of its homaloids.

## o362 citation

## o363 citation

## o364 entry: ordinary

ordinary
An ordinary point of multiplicity m of a curve is one with m distinct tangent lines.

## o365 entry: oscnode

oscnode
A double point of a plane curve that is also a point of osculation; in other words the two branches meet to order at least 3.

## o366 citation

## o367 entry: osculate

osculate
Kiss; to meet with high order. See .

## o368 citation

## o369 entry: osculating plane

osculating plane
A tangent plane of a space curve having third order contact with it.

## o370 entry: outpolar quadric

outpolar quadric
See and

## o371 citation

## o372 citation

## o373 section: P

## o374 entry: Pappus

Pappus
Pappus of Alexandria.
The Pappus configuration is the configuration of 9 lines and 9 points that occurs in Pappus's hexagon theorem.

## o375 entry: parabolic point

parabolic point
A point of a variety that also lies in the Hessian.

## o376 entry: parallel

parallel
Meeting at the line or plane at infinity, as in parallel lines
A parallel curve is the envelope of a circle of fixed radius moving along another curve.

## o377 citation

## o378 entry: partitivity

partitivity
The number of connected components of a real algebraic curve. See .

## o379 citation

## o380 entry: Pascal

Pascal
Short for Pascal line, the line determined by 6 points of a conic in Pascal's theorem

## o381 entry: pedal

pedal
The pedal curve of C with respect to a pedal point P is the locus of points X such that the line through X orthogonal to PX is tangent to C.

## o382 citation

## o383 entry: pencil

pencil
A 1-dimensional linear system. See pencil (mathematics) and Lefschetz pencil.

## o384 entry: pentad

pentad
A set of 5 points

## o385 entry: pentahedron

pentahedron
A union of 5 planes, in particular the Sylvester pentahedron of a cubic surface.

## o386 entry: period

period
The integral of a differential form over a submanifold

## o387 entry: perspectivity

perspectivity
An isomorphism between two projective lines (or ranges) of projective space such that the lines joining each point of one line to the corresponding point of the other line all pass through a fixed point, called the center of the perspectivity or the perspector.

## o388 entry: perspector

perspector
The center of a perspectivity

## o389 entry: perspectrix

perspectrix
The line in Desargues theorem on which the intersections of pairs of sides of two perspective triangles lie

## o390 entry: pinch

pinch
A pinch point is a singular point of a surface, where the two tangent planes of a point on a double curve coincide in a double plane, called the pinch plane.

## o391 citation

## o392 entry: pippian

pippian
Introduced by . Now called the Cayleyan. See also quippian.

## o393 citation

## o394 entry: Plücker

Plücker
For Plücker characteristic see characteristic
A Plücker line is one of the 15 lines containing 4 of the 20 Steiner points associated to 6 points on a conic. The Plücker lines meet in threes at the 60 Kirkman points.

## o395 citation

## o396 entry: plurigenus

plurigenus
Plural plurigenera
The dth plurigenus of a variety is the dimension of the space of sections of the dth power of the canonical line bundle.

## o397 entry: point-star

point-star
A family of lines with a common point

## o398 entry: polar

polar
(Adjective) Related by a polarity
The polar conic is the zero set of the quadratic form associated to a polarity, or equivalently the set of self-conjugate points of the polarity.
(Noun) The first polar, second polar, and so on are varieties of degrees n–1, n–2, ... formed from a point and a hypersurface of degree n by polarizing the equation of the hypersurface.
A polar or polar line is the line corresponding to a point under a polarity of the projective plane.

## o399 citation

## o400 entry: polarity

polarity
A correlation given by a symmetrical matrix, or a correlation of period 2. A polarity of the projective space of a vector space is essentially a non-degenerate symmetric bilinear form, up to multiplication by scalars. See also null-polarity.

## o401 citation

## o402 entry: pole

pole
The point corresponding to a hyperplane under a polarity.
A singularity of a rational function.

## o403 entry: poloconic

poloconic

## o404 entry: polocubic

polocubic

## o405 entry: poloquartic

poloquartic
The poloconic (also called conic polar) of a line in the plane with respect to a cubic curve is the locus of points whose first polar is tangent to the line.

## o406 citation

## o407 entry: polygonal

polygonal
A polygonal (or k-gonal) curve is a curve together with a map (of degree k) to the projective line. The degree of the map is called the gonality of the curve. When the degree is 1, 2, or 3 the curve is called rational, hyperelliptic, or trigonal.

## o408 entry: porism

porism
A porism is a corollary, especially in geometry, as in Poncelet's porism. The precise meaning seems to be controversial.
An arrangement of geometrical figures (such as lines or circles) that are inscribed in one curve and circumscribed around another, as in Poncelet's porism or Steiner's porism. There seems to be some confusion about whether "porism" refers to the geometrical configuration or to the statement of the result.

## o409 entry: poristic

poristic
Having either no solutions or infinitely many . For example, Poncelet's porism and Steiner's porism imply that if there is one way to arrange lines or circles then there are infinitely many ways.

## o410 citation

## o411 entry: postulated

postulated
A postulated object (point, line, and so on) is an object in some larger space. For example, a point at infinity of projective space is a postulated point of affine space.

## o412 citation

## o413 entry: postulation

postulation
The postulation of a variety for some family is the number of independent conditions needed to force an elements of the family to contain the variety.

## o414 citation

## o415 entry: power of a point

power of a point
Laguerre defined the power of a point with respect to an algebraic curve of degree n to be the product of the distances from the point to the intersections with a circle through it, divided by the nth power of the diameter. He showed that this is independent of the choice of circle through the point.

## o416 citation

## o417 entry: prime

prime
An old term for a hyperplane in a projective space.

## o418 citation

## o419 entry: primal

primal
An old term for a projective hypersurface.

## o420 citation

## o421 entry: projectivity

projectivity
An isomorphism between two projective lines (or ranges). A projectivity is a product of at most three perspectivities.

## o422 entry: propinquity

propinquity
A number depending on two branches at a point, defined by .

## o423 citation

## o424 entry: proximate

proximate
For proximate points see .

## o425 citation

## o426 entry: pure

pure
All components are of the same dimension. Now called equidimensional.

## o427 citation

## o428 section: Q

## o429 entry: quadratic transformation

quadratic transformation
A Cremona transformation of degree 2. A standard quadratic transformation is one similar to the map taking each coordinate to its inverse.
A monomial transformation with center a point, or in other words a blowup at a point.

## o430 entry: quadric

quadric
Degree 2, especially a degree 2 projective variety. Not to be confused with quantic or quartic.

## o431 entry: quadrisecant

quadrisecant
A quadrisecant is a line meeting something in four points

## o432 entry: quadro-cubic, quadro-quartic

quadro-cubic, quadro-quartic
A quadro-cubic or quadro-quartic transformation is a Cremona transformation such that the homaloids of the transformation have degree 2 and those of its inverse have degree 3 or 4.

## o433 citation

## o434 entry: quantic

quantic
A homogeneous polynomial in several variables, now usually called a form. Not to be confused with quartic or quadric.

## o435 entry: quarto-quartic

quarto-quartic
A Quarto-quartic transformation is a Cremona transformation such that the homaloids of the transformation and its inverse all have degree 4.

## o436 citation

## o437 entry: quaternary

quaternary
Depending on four variables, as in quaternary form.

## o438 entry: quartic

quartic
Degree 4, especially a degree 4 projective variety. Not to be confused with quantic or quadric.

## o439 entry: quintic

quintic
Degree 5, especially a degree 5 projective variety.

## o440 entry: quippian

quippian
A quippian is a degree 5 class 3 contravariant of a plane cubic introduced by and discussed by . See also pippian.

## o441 citation

## o442 citation

## o443 entry: quotient ring

quotient ring
The quotient ring of a point (or more generally a subvariety) is what is now called its local ring, formed by adding inverses to all functions that do not vanish identically on it.

## o444 section: R

## o445 entry: ramphoid

ramphoid
Beak-like. A ramphoid cusp is one whose two branches curve in the same direction; see keratoid cusp.

## o446 citation

## o447 entry: rank

rank
The rank of a projective curve is the number of tangents to the curve meeting a generic linear subspace of codimension 2.
The rank of a projective surface is the rank of a curve given by the intersection of the surface with a generic hyperplane. See order, class, type.

## o448 citation

## o449 citation

## o450 entry: range

range
The set of all points on a line.
A labeled or finite ordered set of points on a line.

## o451 citation

## o452 entry: rational

rational
Birational to projective space.
Defined over the rational numbers.

## o453 entry: ray

ray
A line, especially one in a family of lines

## o454 entry: regular

regular
A regular surface is one whose irregularity is zero.
Having no singularities; see regular local ring.
Symmetrical, as in regular polygon, regular polyhedron.
Defined everywhere, as in regular (birational) map.

## o455 entry: regulus

regulus
One of the two pencils of lines on a product of two projective planes or a quadric surface.

## o456 entry: related

related
Two ranges (labeled sets) of points on a line are called related if there is a projectivity taking one range to the other.

## o457 entry: representative manifold

representative manifold
A parameter space or moduli space for some family of varieties

## o458 entry: residual

residual
The residual intersection of two varieties consists of the "non-obvious" part of their intersection.

## o459 entry: resultant

resultant
The resultant of two polynomials, given by the determinant of the Sylvester matrix of two binary forms, that vanishes if they have a common root.
A Cremona transformation formed from n correlations of n-dimensional projective space.

## o460 citation

## o461 entry: reverse

reverse
Inverse (of a function or birational map)

## o462 entry: ruled

ruled
Covered by lines, as in ruled surface. See also scroll.

## o463 section: S

## o464 entry: s

s Sn
Projective space of dimension n.

## o465 entry: Salmon conic

Salmon conic
The Salmon conic of a pair of plane conics is the locus of points such that the pairs of tangents to the two conics are harmonically conjugate.

## o466 citation

## o467 entry: satellite

satellite
If a line meets a cubic curve in 3 points, the residual intersections of the tangents of these points with the cubic all lie on a line, called the satellite line of the original line. See .
A certain plane curve of degree (n–1)(n–2) constructed from a plane curve of degree n and a generic point.
For satellite points see . Possibly something to do with base points.

## o468 citation

## o469 citation

## o470 citation

## o471 entry: scroll

scroll
A ruled surface with an embedding into projective space so that the lines of the ruled surface are also lines of projective space.

## o472 entry: secant

secant
A line intersecting a variety in 2 points, or more generally an n-dimensional projective space meeting a variety in n+1 points.
A secant variety is the union of the secants of a variety.

## o473 entry: second kind

second kind
All residues at poles are zero

## o474 entry: secundum

secundum
An intersection of two primes (hyperplanes) in projective space.

## o475 citation

## o476 entry: Segre

Segre
Named after either Beniamino Segre or Corrado Segre
A Segre variety or Segre embedding is the product of two projective spaces, or an embedding of this into a larger projective space.
The Segre cubic is a cubic hypersurface in 4-dimensional projective space.

## o477 entry: self-conjugate

self-conjugate

## o478 entry: self-polar

self-polar
Incident with its image under a polarity. In particular the self-conjugate points of a polarity form the polar conic.
A self-conjugate (or self-polar) triangle (or triad) is a triangle such that each vertex corresponds to the opposite edge under a polarity.
A self-conjugate tetrad is a set of 4 points such that the pole of each side lies on the opposite side.

## o479 citation

## o480 entry: septic

septic

## o481 entry: septimic

septimic
(Adjective) Degree 7
(Noun) A degree 7 projective variety
(Noun) A degree 7 form

## o482 entry: sextactic point

sextactic point
One of the 27 points of an elliptic curve of order dividing 6 but not 3.

## o483 citation

## o484 entry: sextic

sextic
Degree 6, especially a degree 6 projective variety

## o485 entry: simple

simple
A simple point of a variety is a non-singular point. More generally a simple subvariety W of a variety V is one with a regular local ring, which means roughly that most points of W are simple points of V.

## o486 entry: singular

singular
Special in some way, including but not limited to the current sense of having a singularity

## o487 entry: skew

skew
Intersecting in a set that is either empty or of the "expected" dimension. For example skew lines in projective 3-space do not intersect, while skew planes in projective 4-space intersect in a point.

## o488 entry: solid

solid
A 3-dimensional linear subspace of projective space, or in other words the 3-dimensional analogue of a point, line, or plane.

## o489 citation

## o490 entry: special divisor

special divisor
An effective divisor whose first cohomology group (of the associated invertible sheaf) is non-zero.

## o491 entry: spinode

spinode
A cusp. ,

## o492 citation

## o493 citation

## o494 entry: star

star
A collection of lines (and sometimes planes and so on) with a common point, called the center of the star.

## o495 citation

## o496 entry: stationary point

stationary point
A cusp. See .

## o497 citation

## o498 entry: Steiner

Steiner

## o499 entry: Steinerian

Steinerian
Named after Jakob Steiner
A Steinerian is the locus of the singular points of the polar quadrics of a hypersurface.
A Steiner surface is a certain embedding of the projective plane into projective 3-space.
a Steiner point is one of the 20 points lying on 3 of the Pascal lines associated with 6 points on a conic.

## o500 citation

## o501 entry: Steiner–Hessian

Steiner–Hessian
One of Cayley's names for the Cayleyan. See .

## o502 citation

## o503 entry: surface

surface
An abstract surface together with an embedding into projective space.

## o504 entry: superabundance of a divisor on a surface.

superabundance of a divisor on a surface.
The dimension of the first cohomology group of the corresponding sheaf.

## o505 entry: symmetroid

symmetroid
The zeros of the determinant of a symmetric matrix of linear forms

## o506 entry: syntheme

syntheme
A partition of a set of 6 elements into 3 pairs, or an element of the symmetric group on 6 points of cycle shape 222.

## o507 citation

## o508 entry: system

system
A family of algebraic sets in projective space; for example, a line system is a family of lines.

## o509 entry: syzygetic

syzygetic
Paired. Opposite of azygetic, meaning unpaired. Example: syzygetic triad, syzygetic tetrad, syzygetic set, syzygetic pencil.

## o510 entry: syzygy

syzygy
A point is in syzygy with some other points if it is in the linear subspace generated by them. A syzygy is a linear relation between points in an affine space.
An algebraic relation between generators of a ring, especially a ring of invariants or covariants.
A linear relation between generators of a module, or more generally an element of the kernel of a homomorphism of modules.
A global syzygy is a resolution of a module or sheaf.

## o511 citation

## o512 section: T

## o513 entry: tacnode

tacnode
A tacnode is a point of a curve where two branches meet in the same direction.

## o514 citation

## o515 entry: tacnode-cusp

tacnode-cusp
A singularity of a plane curve where a tacnode and a cusp are combined at the same point.

## o516 citation

## o517 entry: tact-invariant

tact-invariant
An invariant of two curves that vanishes if they touch each other. See .

## o518 citation

## o519 entry: tangent cone

tangent cone
A tangent cone is a cone defined by the non-zero terms of smallest degree in the Taylor series at a point of a hypersurface.

## o520 entry: tangential equation

tangential equation
The tangential equation of a plane curve is an equation giving the condition for a line to be tangent to the curve. In other words it is the equation of the dual curve. It is not the equation of a tangent to a curve.

## o521 entry: ternary

ternary
Depending on three variables, as in ternary form

## o522 entry: tetrad

tetrad
A set of 4 points

## o523 entry: tetragram

tetragram
Synonym for complete quadrilateral

## o524 entry: tetrahedroid

tetrahedroid
A tetrahedroid is a special kind of Kummer surface.

## o525 entry: tetrahedron

tetrahedron
A geometric configuration consisting of 4 points and the 6 lines joining pairs. This is similar to the lines and infinite edges of a polyhedral tetrahedron, but in algebraic geometry one sometimes does not include the faces of the tetrahedron.

## o526 entry: tetrastigm

tetrastigm
Synonym for complete quadrangle

## o527 entry: third kind

third kind
All poles are simple (order 1)

## o528 entry: threefold

threefold
(Adjective) Three-dimensional
(Noun) A 3-dimensional variety

## o529 entry: torsal generator.

torsal generator.
A generator of a scroll (ruled surface) that meets its consecutive generator. See .

## o530 citation

## o531 entry: torse

torse
Developable surface.

## o532 entry: transvectant

transvectant
An invariant depending on two forms.

## o533 entry: transversal

transversal
A line meeting several other lines. For example, 4 generic lines in projective 3-space have 2 transversals meeting all of them.

## o534 entry: triad

triad
A set of 3 points

## o535 entry: tricircular

tricircular
A tricircular curve is one that passes through the circular points at infinity with order 3.

## o536 entry: tricuspidal

tricuspidal
Having three cusps

## o537 entry: trigonal

trigonal
A trigonal curve is one with a degree three map to the projective line. See hyperelliptic.

## o538 entry: trihedral

trihedral
A set of 3 planes A Steiner trihedral is a set of three tritangent planes of a cubic surface whose intersection point is not on the surface.

## o539 citation

## o540 entry: trilinear coordinates

trilinear coordinates
Coordinates based on distance from sides of a triangle: Trilinear coordinates.

## o541 entry: trinodal

trinodal
Having three nodes

## o542 entry: tripartite

tripartite
Having three connected components.

## o543 citation

## o544 entry: trisecant

trisecant
A line meeting a variety in 3 points. See trisecant identity.

## o545 entry: tritangent

tritangent
Meeting something in 3 tangent points, such as a tritangent conic to a cubic curve or a tritangent plane of a cubic surface.

## o546 entry: trope

trope
A trope is a singular (meaning special) tangent space. The word is mostly used for a tangent space of a Kummer surface touching it along a conic.

## o547 citation

## o548 entry: twisted

twisted
A twisted cubic is a degree 3 embedding of the projective line in projective 3-space

## o549 entry: total

total
A set of 5 partitions of a 6-element set into three pairs, such that no two elements of the total have a pair in common. For example, {(12)(36)(45), (13)(24)(56), (14)(26)(35), (15)(23)(46), (16)(25)(34)}

## o550 citation

## o551 entry: type

type
The type of a projective surface is the number of tangent planes meeting a generic linear subspace of codimension 4.

## o552 citation

## o553 section: U

## o554 entry: undulation

undulation
A point of undulation of a curve is where the tangent meets the curve to fourth order; also called a hyperflex. See inflection point.

## o555 citation

## o556 entry: unibranch

unibranch
Having only one branch at a point. For example, a cusp of a plane curve is unibranch, while a node is not.

## o557 entry: unicursal

unicursal
A unicursal curve is one that is rational, in other words birational to the projective line. See .

## o558 citation

## o559 entry: unipartite

unipartite
Connected. See

## o560 citation

## o561 entry: unirational

unirational
A correspondence is called unirational if it is generically injective, in other words a rational map.
A variety is called unirational if it is finitely covered by a rational variety.

## o562 citation

## o563 entry: united point

united point
A point in the intersection of the diagonal and a correspondence from a set to itself.

## o564 entry: unode

unode
A double point of a surface whose tangent cone consists of one double plane. See binode.

## o565 section: V

## o566 entry: valence

valence

## o567 entry: valency

valency
The valence or valency of a correspondence T on a curve is a number k such that the divisors T(P)+kP are all linearly equivalent. A correspondence need not have a valency.

## o568 citation

## o569 entry: Veronese surface

Veronese surface
An embedding of the projective plane in 5-dimensional projective space.

## o570 entry: virtual

virtual
An estimate for something that is often but not always correct, such as virtual genus, virtual dimension, and so on. If some number is given by the dimension of a space of sections of some sheaf, the corresponding virtual number is sometimes given by the corresponding Euler characteristic, and equal to the dimension when all higher cohomology groups vanish. See superabundance.

## o571 section: W

## o572 entry: web

web
A 3-dimensional linear system. See "net" and "pencil".

## o573 citation

## o574 entry: Weddle surface

Weddle surface
A quartic surface in projective space given by the locus of the vertex of a cone passing through 6 points in general position.

## o575 entry: Weierstrass point

Weierstrass point
A point on a curve where the dimension of the space of rational functions whose only singularity is a pole of some order at the point is higher than normal.

## o576 entry: Wirtinger sextic

Wirtinger sextic
A degree 4 genus 6 plane curve with nodes at the 6 points of a complete quadrangle.

## o577 section: XYZ

## o578 entry: Zeuthen–Segre invariant

Zeuthen–Segre invariant
The Zeuthen–Segre invariant is 4 less than the Euler characteristic of a non-singular projective surface.
